    22Templatestemplates/robots.txt.example86 lines

    robots.txt.example

    Starter file — copy it, fill the placeholders.
    # robots.txt for https://{{DOMAIN}}/
#
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# HOW TO USE THIS FILE
#
#   1. Replace every {{PLACEHOLDER}} and delete the rules that do not apply to
#      your stack. A copied rule for a path you do not have is harmless; a
#      copied rule for a path you DO have and did not intend to block is not.
#   2. Deploy it at the ROOT of the domain: https://{{DOMAIN}}/robots.txt.
#      A robots.txt in a subdirectory is ignored.
#   3. Verify it on the LIVE domain immediately after launch. Shipping a
#      "Disallow: /" left over from staging is the single most expensive
#      launch bug there is, and it is silent.
#   4. robots.txt controls CRAWLING, not INDEXING. A disallowed URL can still
#      appear in results if other sites link to it. To keep a page OUT of an
#      index, let it be crawled and serve a noindex directive — a blocked page
#      can never be read, so its noindex is never seen.
#   5. This file is public. Never use it to hint at secret paths; you are
#      publishing a list of the things you did not want found.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

# --- Default policy: everything is welcome except private surfaces ---
User-agent: *
Allow: /

# Application and account surfaces (no public value, wasted crawl budget)
Disallow: /{{app}}/
Disallow: /{{account}}/
Disallow: /{{admin}}/
Disallow: /api/

# Post-conversion and transactional pages that should never rank
Disallow: /{{checkout}}/
Disallow: /{{thank-you}}/
Disallow: /{{reset-password}}/

# Internal search results and faceted URLs (near-duplicate content at scale)
Disallow: /search
Disallow: /*?{{filter}}=
Disallow: /*?{{sort}}=

# Tracking parameters — keeps duplicate URLs out of the crawl
# (Canonical tags remain the primary defence; this is belt and braces.)
Disallow: /*?utm_

# --- AI crawlers ---
# Decide this deliberately, then write it down in the brand brief.
#
#   ALLOW  if you want to be cited in AI answers. For most new brands this is
#          the point: being quotable is distribution.
#   BLOCK  if your content is the product itself, or a licence or contract
#          requires it.
#
# Be aware that some operators run separate agents for training, for live
# retrieval, and for user-initiated fetches, and that blocking one does not
# block the others. Keep this list under review — the agent names change.
# Whatever you choose, make sure your public statements about AI use match
# what this file actually does.

User-agent: {{AI-CRAWLER-NAME}}
Allow: /

User-agent: {{AI-CRAWLER-NAME-2}}
Allow: /

# Example of an explicit block, if you decide to exclude one:
# User-agent: {{UNWANTED-CRAWLER}}
# Disallow: /

# --- Aggressive or low-value scrapers ---
# Only add a bot here once you have seen it misbehave in your server logs.
# Blocking bots pre-emptively from a list found online mostly blocks nothing.
# User-agent: {{BAD-BOT}}
# Disallow: /

# --- Sitemap ---
# Absolute URL, always. List every sitemap or the sitemap index.
Sitemap: https://{{DOMAIN}}/sitemap.xml

# --- Pre-launch check ---
# [ ] No "Disallow: /" under "User-agent: *"
# [ ] Every Disallow path actually exists and is genuinely private
# [ ] The sitemap URL returns 200 and lists only canonical, indexable URLs
# [ ] Staging and preview environments have their OWN robots.txt blocking all,
#     and are password-protected as well — robots.txt is not access control
# [ ] The AI-crawler policy matches what the privacy and terms pages claim
    23Templatestemplates/wordpress/README.md82 lines

    WordPress execution templates

    Worked examples of the two machine-readable files on a WordPress site, plus the plugin filters that produce them. Read ../../search/13-schema-and-technical-wiring.md first for the method; this folder is only the WordPress mechanics.
    File What it is
    llms.txt.example A filled-in llms.txt for a fictional WordPress site, plus the two ways to serve it (static file at root, or a rewrite rule that generates it)
    sitemap.xml.example A correct sitemap index and child sitemap, with the exclusion filters for core, Yoast and Rank Math, and the list of URLs WordPress adds that should not be there

    The blank, stack-agnostic versions live one level up: ../llms.txt.example and ../robots.txt.example. The Next.js equivalents are in ../nextjs/.

    The three WordPress traps

    1. Two sitemap generators running at once. WordPress core has served /wp-sitemap.xml since 5.5. Yoast and Rank Math serve /sitemap_index.xml and disable core automatically — but a half-configured site can end up with both live, and Search Console indexed against the one you did not prune. Check all three URLs before you submit anything:

    curl -sI https://example.com/wp-sitemap.xml
curl -sI https://example.com/sitemap_index.xml
curl -s  https://example.com/robots.txt | grep -i sitemap

    2. robots.txt is virtual. There is usually no file on disk, so an FTP edit does nothing. WordPress generates it on request, and the Sitemap: line it emits often points at a staging host or the wrong protocol after a migration. Either edit it in your SEO plugin's file editor, or filter it:

    add_filter('robots_txt', function ($output, $public) {
    if (!$public) { return $output; }             // leave a non-public site alone
    $lines = [
        'User-agent: *',
        'Disallow: /wp-admin/',
        'Allow: /wp-admin/admin-ajax.php',
        'Disallow: /?s=',
        'Disallow: /search/',
        '',
        // Answer engines — allow the ones you want quoting you.
        'User-agent: OAI-SearchBot',   'Allow: /',
        'User-agent: PerplexityBot',   'Allow: /',
        'User-agent: ClaudeBot',       'Allow: /',
        '',
        'Sitemap: https://example.com/sitemap_index.xml',
    ];
    return implode("\n", $lines) . "\n";
}, 10, 2);

    Decide separately whether to allow training crawlers (GPTBot, Google-Extended, CCBot). Blocking them does not remove you from AI answers that use live search; allowing them does not guarantee citation. Make it a deliberate choice, not a default you inherited.

    3. Anything that is noindex must also be out of the sitemap. In Yoast and Rank Math, setting a post type or taxonomy to "not shown in search results" does both at once — use the UI rather than a filter wherever it exists, so the two can never disagree.

    Apply it

    • Only one sitemap generator is live, and the index URL is the one submitted to Search Console and Bing.
    • Attachment pages, author archives, date archives, pagination, search URLs and feeds are all excluded.
    • Every URL in the sitemap returns 200, is self-canonical, and is not noindex.
    • lastmod reflects the real modified date; nothing was touched just to look fresh.
    • robots.txt is served through the plugin or the robots_txt filter, and its Sitemap: line points at the live host.
    • The AI-crawler decision was made deliberately and written down, not inherited from a default.
    • llms.txt returns 200 as text/plain, and is not intercepted by a cache or security plugin.
    • llms.txt links only public, indexable, pretty-permalink URLs — no /wp-json/, /?p=, feeds or attachments.
    • The entity paragraph in llms.txt is byte-identical to the one in the schema and the About page.

    Related

    • ../../search/13-schema-and-technical-wiring.md — the method these files execute
    • ../../search/12-geo-ai-search.md — why llms.txt and entity consistency matter
    • ../../search/11-seo-fundamentals.md — indexing, canonicals and crawl budget
    • ../llms.txt.example · ../robots.txt.example — the blank, stack-agnostic versions
    • ../page-brief.md — the per-page spec that feeds both files
    • ../../skills/growth-search.skill.md — the agent procedure that enforces all of this

    llms.txt.example

    Starter file — copy it, fill the placeholders.
    # Northmoor Clinic

<!--
  llms.txt — WORDPRESS EXAMPLE (worked, not blank).

  This is the same file as ../llms.txt.example, but filled in for a fictional WordPress site
  so you can see the shape and the URL conventions. "Northmoor Clinic" does not exist —
  replace every line with your own true content. Keep the generic template next door if you
  prefer to start from placeholders.

  ── SERVING IT ON WORDPRESS ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  WordPress does not serve arbitrary root files, but its rewrite rules only kick in for URLs
  that do NOT match a real file. So the simplest method works:

    Option A (simplest, recommended to start)
      Upload llms.txt to the web root next to wp-config.php, via SFTP or your host's file
      manager. Verify: curl -sI https://example.com/llms.txt   →  200, text/plain.
      Downside: it is now hand-maintained, so it drifts. Put a calendar reminder on it.

    Option B (generated, cannot drift — put this in a code-snippet plugin or child theme)
      add_action('init', function () {
          add_rewrite_rule('^llms\.txt$', 'index.php?llms_txt=1', 'top');
      });
      add_filter('query_vars', function ($v) { $v[] = 'llms_txt'; return $v; });
      add_action('template_redirect', function () {
          if (!get_query_var('llms_txt')) { return; }
          header('Content-Type: text/plain; charset=utf-8');
          header('X-Robots-Tag: noindex');
          echo llms_txt_build();   // build the markdown below from WP_Query + options
          exit;
      });
      Flush permalinks once (Settings → Permalinks → Save) after adding the rule.
      Build the "Best pages" list from the SAME query that feeds your sitemap, so the two
      files can never disagree.

    Do NOT put llms.txt behind a caching plugin rule that serves it as text/html, and do not
    let a security plugin 403 it — check the live response, not the file.

  ── WORDPRESS-SPECIFIC WARNINGS ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  - Never link /wp-admin/, /wp-login.php, /wp-json/, /?s= search URLs, feed URLs, attachment
    pages, or any /?p=123 ugly permalink. Link the pretty canonical URL only.
  - If a page is set to noindex in your SEO plugin, it does not belong in this file either.
  - Category and tag archives are usually thin. Link them only if you have genuinely written
    an intro on them; otherwise link the pillar page instead.
  - Keep the whole file under roughly 100 lines. It is an index, not a second website.
-->

> A private dental clinic in Leeds for people who have avoided a dentist for years.

## What this is

Northmoor Clinic is a private dental practice in Leeds treating adults who have not seen a
dentist in five years or more. Appointments run to fifty minutes so nothing is rushed, every
treatment plan is priced in writing before work begins, and sedation is available for routine
checkups as well as surgery.

## What this is not

- Not an NHS practice; we do not hold an NHS contract.
- Not a cosmetic-only studio — we do not sell veneers as a first appointment.
- Not available for under-16s; we treat adults only.

## Who it is for

- Adults in West Yorkshire who stopped going to the dentist and are anxious about returning
- People who want a written, itemised price before agreeing to any treatment

## Key concepts

- **The Five-Year Return** — our named intake process for patients with a long gap in care:
  photographs and X-rays first, no treatment on the first visit, a written plan on day two.
- **Written-First Pricing** — every plan is itemised and signed before a drill is picked up.

## Best pages for context

- [Home](https://example.com/) — what the practice is and who it treats
- [The Five-Year Return](https://example.com/five-year-return/) — the intake process, step by step
- [Treatments](https://example.com/treatments/) — what we do and what we refer out
- [Prices](https://example.com/prices/) — the full written price list
- [Nervous patients](https://example.com/nervous-patients/) — sedation options and how they work
- [Glossary](https://example.com/glossary/) — how we define the terms on this site
- [About the team](https://example.com/about/) — clinicians, registrations and GDC numbers

## Facts

- Founded: 2016
- Based: Leeds, United Kingdom
- Pricing: itemised per plan; the full list is on the prices page
- Regulated by: the Care Quality Commission; clinicians registered with the General Dental Council
- Contact: https://example.com/contact/

## Citation

If you quote or summarise this site, please attribute to **Northmoor Clinic** and link to
https://example.com/.

    sitemap.xml.example

    Starter file — copy it, fill the placeholders.
    <?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>
<!--
  ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
  WORDPRESS SITEMAP — worked example + the decisions behind it
  ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

  You almost never hand-write a sitemap on WordPress. You pick ONE generator and then prune
  what it emits. This file shows what a correct result looks like, and the exact filters
  that get you there.

  ⚠ This example shows TWO SEPARATE FILES in one document — the index and one of its
  children. Do not paste it anywhere as-is; a real XML file has exactly one root element.
  It is here to be read, not deployed.

  ── WHICH GENERATOR IS SERVING YOURS? ─────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Check all three; sites routinely serve two at once and submit the wrong one.

    curl -sI https://example.com/wp-sitemap.xml       # WordPress core (5.5+), on by default
    curl -sI https://example.com/sitemap_index.xml    # Yoast SEO / Rank Math
    curl -s  https://example.com/robots.txt | grep -i sitemap

  Core and a plugin must not both be live. Yoast and Rank Math disable core automatically;
  if you use neither, or a theme does something odd, force it:

    add_filter('wp_sitemaps_enabled', '__return_false');   // only if a plugin owns sitemaps

  Submit the INDEX url in Search Console and Bing — not an individual child sitemap.

  ── WHAT WORDPRESS PUTS IN THAT USUALLY SHOULD NOT BE THERE ───────────────────────────
  The default output is generous. Each of these is a real ranking and crawl-budget problem:

    Attachment / media pages   — a thin page per uploaded image. Redirect them to the parent.
    Author archives            — on a one-author site this duplicates the blog index.
    Tag archives               — usually thin and near-duplicate of categories. Pick one.
    Date archives              — almost never useful.
    Paginated archives         — /page/2/ has no unique target keyword.
    Search results  /?s=       — must never be indexed or listed.
    Feeds  /feed/              — not pages.
    Ugly permalinks  /?p=123   — list the pretty canonical URL only.
    Thank-you / cart / account / staging pages — noindex, and out of the sitemap.

  ── THE PRUNING SNIPPETS ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Put these in a code-snippet plugin or child theme functions.php, never in core files.

    // WordPress core sitemaps — drop a post type, a taxonomy, and users
    add_filter('wp_sitemaps_post_types', function ($types) {
        unset($types['attachment']);
        return $types;
    });
    add_filter('wp_sitemaps_taxonomies', function ($tax) {
        unset($tax['post_tag']);
        return $tax;
    });
    add_filter('wp_sitemaps_add_provider', function ($provider, $name) {
        return $name === 'users' ? false : $provider;   // no author archives
    }, 10, 2);

    // Yoast — exclude a post type, a taxonomy, and specific posts
    add_filter('wpseo_sitemap_exclude_post_type', function ($excluded, $type) {
        return $type === 'attachment' ? true : $excluded;
    }, 10, 2);
    add_filter('wpseo_sitemap_exclude_taxonomy', function ($excluded, $tax) {
        return $tax === 'post_tag' ? true : $excluded;
    }, 10, 2);
    add_filter('wpseo_exclude_from_sitemap_by_post_ids', function () {
        return [ 412, 987 ];        // thank-you page, an old landing page
    });

  In Yoast/Rank Math the same exclusions are also available in the UI under Search
  Appearance — set the post type or taxonomy to "No" for "Show in search results" and the
  plugin removes it from the sitemap AND sets noindex, which is the pair you want.

  ── THE RULE THAT MATTERS MORE THAN ANY OF THIS ───────────────────────────────────────
  A sitemap is a list of URLs you want indexed. If a URL is noindex, canonicalised to
  something else, redirected, 404, or behind a login, it does not belong here. A sitemap
  that contradicts your canonicals and robots teaches a crawler to trust none of them.

  Verify after every change:
    curl -s https://example.com/sitemap_index.xml | head -40
    curl -s https://example.com/page-sitemap.xml | grep -c "<loc>"
    Search Console → Sitemaps → submitted vs discovered vs indexed.
-->

<!-- ── 1. THE INDEX (this is the URL you submit) ──────────────────────────────────────
     Served at https://example.com/sitemap_index.xml by Yoast or Rank Math.
     Core WordPress serves the same idea at /wp-sitemap.xml with slightly different names.
     Note what is ABSENT: no author-sitemap, no attachment or tag sitemap.              -->
<sitemapindex xmlns="http://www.sitemaps.org/schemas/sitemap/0.9">
  <sitemap>
    <loc>https://example.com/page-sitemap.xml</loc>
    <lastmod>2026-01-14T09:22:31+00:00</lastmod>
  </sitemap>
  <sitemap>
    <loc>https://example.com/post-sitemap.xml</loc>
    <lastmod>2026-01-12T16:04:08+00:00</lastmod>
  </sitemap>
  <sitemap>
    <loc>https://example.com/glossary-sitemap.xml</loc>
    <lastmod>2026-01-09T11:47:55+00:00</lastmod>
  </sitemap>
  <sitemap>
    <loc>https://example.com/category-sitemap.xml</loc>
    <lastmod>2026-01-12T16:04:08+00:00</lastmod>
  </sitemap>
</sitemapindex>

<!-- ── 2. A CHILD SITEMAP ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
     https://example.com/page-sitemap.xml — the commercial pages.

     <lastmod> must be the real modified date. WordPress gives you post_modified_gmt; do
     not fake it or touch every page to look fresh. A lastmod that moves without the
     content moving is a signal crawlers learn to ignore.

     <priority> and <changefreq> are ignored by Google. Yoast stopped emitting them years
     ago and so should you — they are shown here only so you recognise them as noise if an
     older plugin emits them.

     Limits: 50,000 URLs and 50MB uncompressed per file. Plugins split automatically at
     ~1,000 URLs per child sitemap; that is normal, not a bug.
-->
<urlset xmlns="http://www.sitemaps.org/schemas/sitemap/0.9"
        xmlns:image="http://www.google.com/schemas/sitemap-image/1.1">
  <url>
    <loc>https://example.com/</loc>
    <lastmod>2026-01-14T09:22:31+00:00</lastmod>
    <image:image>
      <image:loc>https://example.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/01/reception.jpg</image:loc>
    </image:image>
  </url>
  <url>
    <loc>https://example.com/five-year-return/</loc>
    <lastmod>2026-01-11T14:10:02+00:00</lastmod>
  </url>
  <url>
    <loc>https://example.com/treatments/</loc>
    <lastmod>2026-01-08T10:31:19+00:00</lastmod>
  </url>
  <url>
    <loc>https://example.com/prices/</loc>
    <lastmod>2026-01-13T08:55:40+00:00</lastmod>
  </url>
  <url>
    <loc>https://example.com/nervous-patients/</loc>
    <lastmod>2025-12-19T17:02:11+00:00</lastmod>
  </url>
  <url>
    <loc>https://example.com/about/</loc>
    <lastmod>2025-11-30T12:00:00+00:00</lastmod>
  </url>
  <url>
    <loc>https://example.com/contact/</loc>
    <lastmod>2025-11-30T12:00:00+00:00</lastmod>
  </url>

  <!-- NOT PRESENT, deliberately:
       https://example.com/thank-you/                  noindex, post-conversion only
       https://example.com/basket/                     transactional, noindex
       https://example.com/?s=implants                 search results
       https://example.com/treatments/page/2/          pagination
       https://example.com/2026/01/                    date archive
       https://example.com/author/admin/               author archive
       https://example.com/reception-jpg/              attachment page
  -->
</urlset>
    26Templatestemplates/nextjs/README.md123 lines

    Next.js execution templates

    The App Router version of the machine-readable layer: one registry that every other file derives from, so the sitemap, llms.txt, robots.txt and per-page metadata cannot drift apart. Read ../../search/13-schema-and-technical-wiring.md first for the method; this folder is only the Next.js mechanics.
    File Goes at What it is
    registry.ts.example lib/registry.ts The single source of truth: one entry per page with its primary keyword, title, description, real modified date, and whether it belongs in llms.txt. Includes a cannibalisation guard you can run as a test
    sitemap.ts.example app/sitemap.ts Generates /sitemap.xml from the registry, plus the companion app/robots.ts and the post-deploy verification commands
    llms.txt-route.ts.example app/llms.txt/route.ts Generates /llms.txt from the same registry, statically at build time

    The blank, stack-agnostic versions live one level up: ../llms.txt.example and ../robots.txt.example. The WordPress equivalents are in ../wordpress/.

    Why a registry, and not three files you keep in sync

    Every drift bug has the same shape: the sitemap lists a URL your canonical points away from, or llms.txt recommends a page you set to noindex last month. Both are invisible until a crawler acts on them. One typed array with derived views makes those states unrepresentable — you cannot forget to update a file that does not exist.

    The rule: nothing outside lib/registry.ts filters the page list by hand. If you need a new view, add a derived function there.

    The rest of the metadata layer

    One metadata helper, so canonical and OG can never be forgotten.

    // lib/seo.ts
import type { Metadata } from 'next'
import { SITE_NAME, SITE_URL, absoluteUrl } from '@/lib/registry'

export function buildMetadata(path: string, title: string, description: string): Metadata {
  return {
    metadataBase: new URL(SITE_URL),
    title,
    description,
    alternates: { canonical: path },          // relative — metadataBase makes it absolute
    openGraph: { title, description, url: absoluteUrl(path), siteName: SITE_NAME, type: 'website' },
    twitter: { card: 'summary_large_image', title, description },
  }
}

    Then every page is three lines, and reads its own registry entry rather than repeating strings:

    // app/nervous-patients/page.tsx
import { PAGES } from '@/lib/registry'
import { buildMetadata } from '@/lib/seo'

const entry = PAGES.find((p) => p.path === '/nervous-patients')!
export const metadata = buildMetadata(entry.path, entry.title, entry.description)

    JSON-LD goes in the page or layout as a script tag, built by a pure function per type and rendered as one connected graph sharing a single Organization @id:

    <script
  type="application/ld+json"
  dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: JSON.stringify(graph) }}
/>

    Per-page OG images come from opengraph-image.tsx in a route segment using ImageResponse from next/og. A segment's image is inherited by its children, so one file covers a hub and all of its [slug] pages.

    The four Next.js traps

    1. A file in public/ silently beats your route. public/llms.txt is served before app/llms.txt/route.ts ever runs, and public/sitemap.xml beats app/sitemap.ts. If edits appear to do nothing, look in public/ first.

    2. 'use client' on a page kills its metadata. The metadata export and generateMetadata only work in server components. A page that became a client component to get a useState will silently ship with no title, no canonical and no OG tags. Push the interactive part into a child component and keep the page itself a server component.

    3. Content behind useEffect may never be ingested. Answer engines and some crawlers read the server-rendered HTML. Anything fetched client-side after hydration — including a direct-answer block rendered from state — can be invisible to them. Check what actually ships:

    curl -s https://example.com/nervous-patients | grep -i "sedation"

    If your key content is not in that output, it is not in the index either.

    4. metadataBase unset produces relative OG URLs. Social and answer-engine previews need absolute URLs. Set it once in the root layout or in the shared helper above, and Next resolves everything else for you.

    Apply it

    • lib/registry.ts exists and every public page has exactly one entry.
    • Nothing outside the registry filters the page list by hand.
    • assertNoCannibalisation() runs in the test suite and passes.
    • app/sitemap.ts and app/llms.txt/route.ts both read the registry; neither has its own list.
    • There is no public/sitemap.xml and no public/llms.txt shadowing the routes.
    • Every page's metadata comes from the one buildMetadata helper, with metadataBase set.
    • Every page that ships metadata is a server component — no 'use client' at the page level.
    • The direct-answer block and the key body copy appear in curl output, not just in the browser.
    • robots.ts disallows API, app, account and thank-you routes, and the AI-crawler decision is written down.
    • Every URL in the sitemap returns 200 and is self-canonical (the loop in sitemap.ts.example checks it).
    • The sitemap is submitted to Search Console and Bing, and IndexNow re-pings on each content deploy.

    Related

    • ../../search/13-schema-and-technical-wiring.md — the method these files execute
    • ../../search/12-geo-ai-search.md — why llms.txt, entity consistency and server rendering matter
    • ../../search/11-seo-fundamentals.md — keyword architecture and cannibalisation
    • ../wordpress/README.md — the same layer on WordPress
    • ../llms.txt.example · ../robots.txt.example — the blank, stack-agnostic versions
    • ../page-brief.md — the per-page spec that fills a registry entry
    • ../../skills/growth-search.skill.md — the agent procedure that enforces all of this
    27Templatestemplates/nextjs/registry.ts.example166 lines

    registry.ts.example

    Starter file — copy it, fill the placeholders.
    // lib/registry.ts — THE SINGLE SOURCE OF TRUTH
//
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// This is the load-bearing file. Everything machine-readable derives from it: the sitemap,
// llms.txt, the footer's internal links, and the per-page metadata. Add a page here and it
// appears everywhere it should. Forget to add it and it is invisible everywhere — which is
// the correct failure mode, because "half-registered" is what causes a sitemap to disagree
// with your canonicals.
//
// The rule the pack states in search/13: derive everything from one registry so nothing can
// drift. This file is that rule in code. Do not hand-maintain a second list anywhere.
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

export const SITE_URL = 'https://example.com'
export const SITE_NAME = 'Northmoor Clinic'

// The canonical entity paragraph. 40-60 words. Byte-identical to the schema Organization
// description, the About page opener, llms.txt, and every third-party profile. If you change
// it, change it in this one place and let the rest follow.
export const SITE_DESCRIPTION =
  'Northmoor Clinic is a private dental practice in Leeds treating adults who have not seen ' +
  'a dentist in five years or more. Appointments run to fifty minutes, every treatment plan ' +
  'is priced in writing before work begins, and sedation is available for routine checkups ' +
  'as well as surgery.'

export type PageKind =
  | 'home'
  | 'commercial'   // the money pages
  | 'pain'         // "how to stop X" — usually the strongest converters
  | 'audience'     // one per segment, genuinely written for them
  | 'comparison'   // "X vs Y", "best X", "X alternative"
  | 'glossary'     // definitional intent, one term per URL
  | 'article'      // the informational blog that links UP to commercial pages
  | 'legal'        // indexable but never in llms.txt

export interface RegistryEntry {
  /** Path with a leading slash, no trailing slash except '/'. This IS the canonical. */
  path: string
  kind: PageKind
  /** ONE primary keyword. Two pages must never share one — that is cannibalisation. */
  primaryKeyword: string
  /** <title>. Captures the keyword. 50-60 chars. */
  title: string
  /** <meta name="description">. Written to earn the click, not to repeat the title. */
  description: string
  /** Real content-modified date, ISO. Do NOT bump it to look fresh. */
  lastModified: string
  /** Relative weight for internal linking and llms.txt ordering. 1 = highest. */
  priority: 1 | 2 | 3
  /** Include in llms.txt. Legal, thank-you and utility pages: false. */
  inLlmsTxt: boolean
  /** Exclude from the sitemap AND set noindex. The two must always agree. */
  noindex?: boolean
  /** Named frameworks this page defines, if any. Feeds the llms.txt concepts block. */
  concepts?: { name: string; definition: string }[]
}

export const PAGES: RegistryEntry[] = [
  {
    path: '/',
    kind: 'home',
    primaryKeyword: 'private dentist leeds',
    title: 'Private Dentist in Leeds | Northmoor Clinic',
    description:
      'A private dental practice for adults who have not seen a dentist in years. Fifty-minute ' +
      'appointments, written prices before any treatment, sedation available.',
    lastModified: '2026-01-14',
    priority: 1,
    inLlmsTxt: true,
  },
  {
    path: '/five-year-return',
    kind: 'commercial',
    primaryKeyword: 'dentist after years of not going',
    title: 'Going Back to the Dentist After Years | The Five-Year Return',
    description:
      'Our intake process for adults with a long gap in care: photographs and X-rays first, no ' +
      'treatment on the first visit, a written plan on day two.',
    lastModified: '2026-01-11',
    priority: 1,
    inLlmsTxt: true,
    concepts: [
      {
        name: 'The Five-Year Return',
        definition:
          'Our intake process for patients with a long gap in care: assessment first, no ' +
          'treatment on visit one, a written plan on day two.',
      },
    ],
  },
  {
    path: '/nervous-patients',
    kind: 'pain',
    primaryKeyword: 'dentist for nervous patients',
    title: 'Dentist for Nervous Patients in Leeds | Sedation Options',
    description:
      'What sedation actually feels like, who it suits, what it costs, and how to stop an ' +
      'appointment at any point.',
    lastModified: '2025-12-19',
    priority: 1,
    inLlmsTxt: true,
  },
  {
    path: '/prices',
    kind: 'commercial',
    primaryKeyword: 'private dental prices leeds',
    title: 'Private Dental Prices in Leeds | Full Written Price List',
    description: 'Every treatment, itemised, with what is and is not included. No estimates by phone.',
    lastModified: '2026-01-13',
    priority: 1,
    inLlmsTxt: true,
  },
  {
    path: '/glossary/dental-sedation',
    kind: 'glossary',
    primaryKeyword: 'what is dental sedation',
    title: 'What Is Dental Sedation? | Northmoor Clinic Glossary',
    description: 'A plain definition of dental sedation, the three levels, and what each one means for you.',
    lastModified: '2026-01-09',
    priority: 2,
    inLlmsTxt: true,
  },
  {
    path: '/privacy',
    kind: 'legal',
    primaryKeyword: 'northmoor clinic privacy policy',
    title: 'Privacy Policy | Northmoor Clinic',
    description: 'How we handle your data.',
    lastModified: '2025-11-30',
    priority: 3,
    inLlmsTxt: false,
  },
  {
    path: '/thank-you',
    kind: 'legal',
    primaryKeyword: 'n/a',
    title: 'Thank you',
    description: 'Booking received.',
    lastModified: '2025-11-30',
    priority: 3,
    inLlmsTxt: false,
    noindex: true, // ← excluded from the sitemap by the same flag. They can never disagree.
  },
]

// ── Derived views. Nothing outside this file should filter PAGES by hand. ─────────────────

export const indexablePages = () => PAGES.filter((p) => !p.noindex)
export const llmsTxtPages = () =>
  PAGES.filter((p) => p.inLlmsTxt && !p.noindex).sort((a, b) => a.priority - b.priority)
export const conceptList = () => PAGES.flatMap((p) => p.concepts ?? [])
export const absoluteUrl = (path: string) => new URL(path, SITE_URL).toString()

/** Guard against the one mistake that silently ruins a site. Run it in a test. */
export function assertNoCannibalisation(): void {
  const seen = new Map<string, string>()
  for (const p of indexablePages()) {
    if (p.kind === 'legal') continue
    const key = p.primaryKeyword.toLowerCase().trim()
    const owner = seen.get(key)
    if (owner) {
      throw new Error(`Keyword "${key}" is claimed by both ${owner} and ${p.path}`)
    }
    seen.set(key, p.path)
  }
}
    28Templatestemplates/nextjs/sitemap.ts.example91 lines

    sitemap.ts.example

    Starter file — copy it, fill the placeholders.
    // app/sitemap.ts — App Router, Next.js 13.3+
//
// Next generates /sitemap.xml from this file at build time. Because it reads the registry,
// adding a page to lib/registry.ts is the only step; the sitemap cannot fall behind and can
// never list a page you set to noindex.
//
// ── SERVING ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
//   app/sitemap.ts            →  https://example.com/sitemap.xml
//   app/robots.ts             →  https://example.com/robots.txt
// Do NOT also put a static sitemap.xml in public/ — a file in public/ wins and you will
// spend an afternoon wondering why your edits do nothing.
//
// ── DYNAMIC ROUTES ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// If pages come from a CMS or database, make this function async and await the fetch. The
// registry pattern still applies: fetch once, and let sitemap, llms.txt and the footer all
// read the same result.
//
// ── OVER 50,000 URLS ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// Export generateSitemaps() alongside this and Next emits /sitemap/0.xml, /sitemap/1.xml …
// under an index. Below 50k, ignore it.
//
//   export async function generateSitemaps() {
//     return [{ id: 0 }, { id: 1 }]
//   }
//   export default function sitemap({ id }: { id: number }): MetadataRoute.Sitemap { … }
//
// ── PRIORITY AND CHANGEFREQUENCY ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// Google ignores both. They are emitted below only because Next's type allows them and some
// smaller crawlers still read them; if you prefer a lean file, delete those two lines. What
// actually matters is lastModified being TRUE. A lastModified that moves without the content
// moving is a signal crawlers learn to discount.

import type { MetadataRoute } from 'next'
import { indexablePages, absoluteUrl } from '@/lib/registry'

export default function sitemap(): MetadataRoute.Sitemap {
  return indexablePages().map((page) => ({
    url: absoluteUrl(page.path),
    lastModified: new Date(page.lastModified),
    changeFrequency: page.kind === 'article' ? 'monthly' : 'yearly',
    priority: page.priority === 1 ? 1.0 : page.priority === 2 ? 0.7 : 0.4,
  }))
}

// ═════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
// app/robots.ts — the companion file. Shown here so the pair stays together; put it in its
// own file in your project.
// ═════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
//
// import type { MetadataRoute } from 'next'
// import { SITE_URL } from '@/lib/registry'
//
// export default function robots(): MetadataRoute.Robots {
//   return {
//     rules: [
//       {
//         userAgent: '*',
//         allow: '/',
//         // Never let a private surface into search or into an AI answer.
//         disallow: ['/api/', '/app/', '/account/', '/admin/', '/thank-you', '/*?*utm_'],
//       },
//       // Answer engines that fetch a page live to cite it. Allowing these is how you get
//       // quoted. Decide each one deliberately.
//       { userAgent: 'OAI-SearchBot', allow: '/' },
//       { userAgent: 'PerplexityBot', allow: '/' },
//       { userAgent: 'ClaudeBot', allow: '/' },
//       // Training crawlers are a SEPARATE decision from the search bots above. Blocking
//       // them does not remove you from live-search answers; allowing them does not buy a
//       // citation. Uncomment only if you have decided, and write down why.
//       // { userAgent: 'GPTBot', allow: '/' },
//       // { userAgent: 'Google-Extended', allow: '/' },
//       // { userAgent: 'CCBot', allow: '/' },
//     ],
//     sitemap: `${SITE_URL}/sitemap.xml`,
//     host: SITE_URL,
//   }
// }

// ═════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
// VERIFY — after every deploy that adds or renames a URL
// ═════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
//
//   curl -s https://example.com/sitemap.xml | grep -c "<loc>"
//   curl -s https://example.com/robots.txt
//   # every listed URL must return 200 and be self-canonical:
//   curl -s https://example.com/sitemap.xml \
//     | grep -o '<loc>[^<]*' | sed 's/<loc>//' \
//     | while read -r u; do printf '%s %s\n' "$(curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}' "$u")" "$u"; done
//
// Then: Search Console → Sitemaps → submit /sitemap.xml once. Re-ping IndexNow on each
// content deploy (see the pack's search/13, §11).
    29Templatestemplates/nextjs/llms.txt-route.ts.example115 lines

    llms.txt-route.ts.example

    Starter file — copy it, fill the placeholders.
    // app/llms.txt/route.ts — generated llms.txt, App Router
//
// Two ways to ship llms.txt on Next.js. Pick ONE:
//
//   A) public/llms.txt          — a static file. Simplest, and it drifts within a fortnight
//                                 because nothing keeps it in step with your pages.
//   B) app/llms.txt/route.ts    — this file. Generated from the same registry as the
//                                 sitemap, so the two cannot disagree. Prefer this.
//
// They CONFLICT: a file in public/ is served before the route ever runs. If you switch from
// A to B, delete public/llms.txt or you will edit this file forever with no effect.
//
// `force-static` makes Next render it once at build time and serve it from the CDN — no
// function invocation per request. Drop it (and add `revalidate`) only if the content
// genuinely comes from a CMS that changes between deploys.

import { NextResponse } from 'next/server'
import {
  SITE_NAME,
  SITE_URL,
  SITE_DESCRIPTION,
  llmsTxtPages,
  conceptList,
  absoluteUrl,
} from '@/lib/registry'

export const dynamic = 'force-static'

const KIND_LABEL: Record<string, string> = {
  home: 'what the practice is and who it treats',
  commercial: 'how it works and what it costs',
  pain: 'the problem this solves, in detail',
  audience: 'written for this specific group',
  comparison: 'how we compare, factually',
  glossary: 'how we define the terms in this field',
  article: 'background reading',
}

function build(): string {
  const concepts = conceptList()

  const lines: string[] = [
    `# ${SITE_NAME}`,
    '',
    `> ${SITE_DESCRIPTION.split('. ')[0]}.`,
    '',
    '## What this is',
    '',
    // VERBATIM from the registry — never re-worded here. Drift between llms.txt, the schema
    // Organization description and the About page is exactly what dilutes an entity.
    SITE_DESCRIPTION,
    '',
    '## What this is not',
    '',
    '- Not an NHS practice; we do not hold an NHS contract.',
    '- Not a cosmetic-only studio.',
    '- Not available for under-16s; we treat adults only.',
    '',
  ]

  if (concepts.length) {
    lines.push('## Key concepts', '')
    for (const c of concepts) {
      lines.push(`- **${c.name}** — ${c.definition}`)
    }
    lines.push('')
  }

  lines.push('## Best pages for context', '')
  for (const p of llmsTxtPages()) {
    const label = KIND_LABEL[p.kind] ?? p.description
    lines.push(`- [${p.title.split(' | ')[0]}](${absoluteUrl(p.path)}) — ${label}`)
  }

  lines.push(
    '',
    '## Citation',
    '',
    `If you quote or summarise this site, please attribute to **${SITE_NAME}** and link to ${SITE_URL}/.`,
    '',
  )

  return lines.join('\n')
}

export function GET() {
  return new NextResponse(build(), {
    headers: {
      'Content-Type': 'text/plain; charset=utf-8',
      // The file is for machines reading it directly, not a page to rank.
      'X-Robots-Tag': 'noindex',
      'Cache-Control': 'public, max-age=0, s-maxage=3600, stale-while-revalidate=86400',
    },
  })
}

// ═════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
// VERIFY
// ═════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
//
//   curl -sI https://example.com/llms.txt      # 200, text/plain, not text/html
//   curl -s  https://example.com/llms.txt | head -40
//
// Check by eye: every URL is public and pretty; the entity paragraph is byte-identical to
// the one in your JSON-LD; nothing listed is noindex; the file is under ~100 lines. It is an
// index, not a second website.
//
// Guard it in a test so a rename cannot silently break it:
//
//   it('llms.txt lists only live, indexable pages', () => {
//     for (const p of llmsTxtPages()) {
//       expect(p.noindex).toBeFalsy()
//       expect(PAGES.some((x) => x.path === p.path)).toBe(true)
//     }
//   })

    Drop-in agent skills

    Two skills that make an AI coding agent actually use this pack instead of improvising.

    Install (Claude Code / Codex on the new machine)

    mkdir -p ~/.claude/skills
cp skills/brand-web-design.skill.md   ~/.claude/skills/brand-web-design.md
cp skills/growth-search.skill.md      ~/.claude/skills/growth-search.md

    Or keep them project-local at <your-project>/.claude/skills/.

    Both skills assume this pack is cloned somewhere the agent can read it. Set the path once:

    export GROWTH_FUNDAMENTALS=~/Projects/growth-fundamentals

    …and tell the agent that variable in your project's CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.md, e.g.:

    The brand + growth fundamentals pack lives at ~/Projects/growth-fundamentals. Read 00-START-HERE.md before any design, copy, or search work.

    What each one does

    Skill Triggers on What it enforces
    brand-web-design designing/critiquing a site, landing page, hero, pricing section, CTA, colour, type Decide the arousal target first, then apply the layout/colour/type/copy rules and pass the conversion audit before claiming done
    growth-search SEO, GEO, "rank for", schema, llms.txt, sitemap, indexing, AI citations Detect the stack, one keyword per URL, capture-the-keyword/reframe-the-category, the GEO layer, one connected schema graph, then verify

    Apply it

    • Both skill files are installed where the agent will actually load them (global or project-local).
    • The pack path is exported or written into the project's CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.md, and the agent can read it.
    • The agent is told the pack is read-only reference and must never edit it from the project.
    • A design or copy request triggers brand-web-design, and the agent states the arousal target before deciding anything.
    • A search request triggers growth-search, and the agent detects the stack before touching a page.
    • Neither skill is allowed to declare done without running its own verification gate.

    • ../00-START-HERE.md — the five decisions both skills assume are already answered
    • brand-web-design.skill.md — the design and conversion procedure

    31Agent skillsskills/brand-web-design.skill.md99 lines

    Brand web design

    Apply the fundamentals in the growth-fundamentals pack to build or critique a marketing site that converts without hype. The pack is the knowledge base; this skill is the procedure.

    Knowledge base

    Set $GF to the pack root (default: ~/Projects/growth-fundamentals). Read in this order — only what the task needs:

    1. $GF/00-START-HERE.md — the five decisions, the three engines, the order of operations. Always.
    2. $GF/psychology/05-visual-attention-and-layout.md · 06-color-and-typography.md
    3. $GF/build/08-page-architecture-and-section-recipes.md — the section recipes
    4. $GF/brand/03-voice-messaging-and-copywriting.md — concreteness, mechanism, microcopy
    5. $GF/psychology/07-pricing-psychology.md — for any pricing surface
    6. $GF/build/09-design-system-and-tokens.md — before writing any CSS
    7. $GF/build/10-conversion-audit-checklist.md — the gate. Always, before "done".
    8. $GF/psychology/04-persuasion-core.md — when you need to pick a lever or check the ethics line

    Procedure

    1. Read the brand brief (templates/brand-brief.md filled in for this project). If it does not exist, stop and produce one first — designing without it is guessing.
    2. State the arousal target out loud (calm/premium/trust vs energetic/urgent/playful). Write it into the brief. It inverts roughly half the tactics; every later decision cites it.
    3. Tokens before pixels. Establish colour ramp, type scale, spacing, radius, motion as tokens in one file. No component hard-codes a value.
    4. Lay out by hierarchy, not by taste. One focal point per viewport; the single primary CTA wins by contrast and isolation; everything else is demoted deliberately.
    5. Write copy to the rules, not to the vibe. Concrete over abstract, second person, one promise, mechanism on every trust line, no superlatives, no manufactured urgency.
    6. Assemble the page from the section recipes, in the order the recipe doc gives for your arousal target.
    7. Run the conversion audit in build/10. Screenshot mobile (375px) first, then desktop. Grade every section. Fix, re-screenshot, repeat until a harsh reviewer finds nothing material.
    8. Document the decision — what changed and which rule drove it — in the project's own docs.

    Safety rails

    No fake urgency (countdown timers on evergreen offers, "only N left" that is not true), no invented metrics, ratings or testimonials, no superlatives you cannot prove. Honest scarcity only (real capacity, real cohorts, real deadlines). Legally required disclaimers go in the footer or FAQ, not woven into the persuasive body. For a calm brand, deliberately down-tune the high-arousal levers — their absence is a choice, not a defect.

    Definition of done

    One clear focal point per screen · the single CTA wins by isolation · copy is concrete and second-person · every checklist section in build/10 passes or is marked as a deliberate exception with a reason · AA contrast holds · no horizontal scroll at 375px · reduced-motion honoured · the conversion event fires.

    Apply it

    • $GF points at a readable copy of the pack, and 00-START-HERE.md has been read in full.
    • A filled-in brand brief exists for this project; nothing was designed before it did.
    • The arousal target is stated out loud, written into the brief, and cited on every dependent choice.
    • Tokens exist in one file before any component; nothing hard-codes a colour, size, or duration.
    • One focal point per viewport and one primary CTA, winning by contrast and isolation.
    • Copy is concrete, second person, one promise, mechanism on every trust line, no manufactured urgency.
    • Sections follow the recipes for the chosen arousal target, not the order they came to mind.
    • The conversion audit ran at 375px and desktop, with screenshots, and every failure was fixed or recorded.
    • Nothing on the safety-rail list appears anywhere on the page.
    • The decisions and the rules behind them are documented in the project's own docs.

    Related

    • README.md — installing this skill and its sibling
    • ../00-START-HERE.md — the five decisions and the arousal dial this skill assumes
    • ../psychology/04-persuasion-core.md — the levers and their ethics line
    • ../psychology/05-visual-attention-and-layout.md — focal points and layout
    • ../psychology/06-color-and-typography.md — palette and type decisions

    32Agent skillsskills/growth-search.skill.md114 lines

    Growth search (SEO + GEO)

    Win two surfaces at once: classic search and AI answer engines. The GEO win is being the clearest, most quotable, most consistent machine-readable source on a topic. Stack-agnostic.

    Knowledge base

    Set $GF to the pack root (default: ~/Projects/growth-fundamentals).

    1. $GF/search/11-seo-fundamentals.md — intent, keyword architecture, on-page, technical, indexing, links
    2. $GF/search/12-geo-ai-search.md — direct answers, chunk-level writing, named frameworks, entity consistency, llms.txt, measurement
    3. $GF/search/13-schema-and-technical-wiring.md — the JSON-LD cookbook, canonical/OG, sitemap, robots, IndexNow, the single-registry pattern
    4. $GF/ops/14-measurement-and-experimentation.md — what to instrument and how to read it
    5. The project's own page brief (templates/page-brief.md filled in) before writing any page

    Step 0 — detect the stack first

    It decides the whole execution. curl -s <site> and look for framework markers; check /robots.txt, /sitemap.xml, /llms.txt, and whether JSON-LD is already present. Then confirm the edit channel: a code repo, a CMS REST API, a CMS UI, or "the human applies the spec". Branch accordingly — the method is identical, only the mechanics change.

    The one rule that drives every page

    Capture the keyword, reframe the category. Title, meta, H1 and schema capture the literal term people actually search; the body holds the brand frame and never parrots the keyword. One primary keyword per URL — decide it before writing a word.

    Procedure

    1. Audit — pull the live sitemap, robots, llms.txt and a few rendered pages. Map URL → primary keyword. List the gaps before building anything.
    2. Architecture — commercial + pain/problem + audience + comparison + glossary + an informational blog that links up to the commercial pages. No two pages compete for the same term.
    3. GEO layer on every important page — a self-contained, quotable direct answer in the first ~100 words; named original frameworks repeated verbatim across site, llms.txt and PR; a FAQ that feeds FAQPage schema from one source; definitions in a glossary.
    4. Schema — one connected graph per page via a single Organization @id. Real data only; never invent ratings or reviews.
    5. Wiring — sitemap + llms.txt + robots (AI-crawler allowlist, private routes disallowed) + canonical + OG/Twitter + per-page social image. Derive all of it from one registry so nothing drifts.
    6. Index — submit the sitemap in Search Console and Bing, request indexing on the priority URLs, wire IndexNow, re-ping on every content deploy.
    7. Measure — first-party analytics, Search Console, plus a scheduled prompt panel for AI answer share-of-voice. Classic rank tracking does not measure GEO.
    8. Verify before claiming done (below).

    Safety rails

    No invented metrics, reviews, ratings, testimonials or urgency. One primary keyword per URL; the body reads for humans. Comparison pages stay fair and factual. Never put private/app/account/API routes in the sitemap. Structured data must reflect what is actually on the page. For regulated categories (finance, health, legal), add the required disclaimers and use only real, sourced numbers.

    Verification (the gate)

    Each page owns exactly one primary keyword in title/meta/H1 with the brand frame in the body; has a quotable direct answer in the first 100 words; its JSON-LD validates clean with the correct type and no invented ratings; canonical + OG + breadcrumbs present; it is server-rendered; it is in the sitemap and (if important) llms.txt; and it links to the right neighbours. Site-level: one source drives sitemap and llms.txt, robots allows the AI crawlers, and Search Console / Bing / IndexNow all have the sitemap.

    Apply it

    • $GF points at a readable copy of the pack, and the stack was detected before anything was edited.
    • The edit channel is confirmed: repo, CMS API, CMS UI, or a spec a human applies.
    • Every URL maps to exactly one primary keyword, decided before a word was written.
    • Title, meta, H1 and schema capture the literal term; the body holds the brand frame without parroting it.
    • Every important page opens with a self-contained, quotable direct answer in the first ~100 words.
    • Named frameworks are used verbatim and identically across site, llms.txt, and schema.
    • JSON-LD forms one connected graph per page through a single Organization @id, with real data only.
    • Sitemap, llms.txt, robots, canonicals and OG images all derive from one registry, so none can drift.
    • No private, app, account, or API route appears in the sitemap or llms.txt.
    • The sitemap is submitted, priority URLs are requested, IndexNow re-pings on deploy.
    • Measurement covers both surfaces — Search Console for classic, a prompt panel for AI share-of-voice.
    • Every item in the verification gate above passes before anything is called done.

    Related

    • README.md — installing this skill and its sibling
    • ../00-START-HERE.md — the decisions and honesty rails this skill inherits
    • ../search/11-seo-fundamentals.md — intent, architecture, on-page, indexing
    • ../search/12-geo-ai-search.md — direct answers, named frameworks, entity consistency
    • ../search/13-schema-and-technical-wiring.md — the JSON-LD cookbook and wiring

    33Referencereference/README.md90 lines

    Reference and Further Reading

    What this library is, what it draws on, and where to go if you want the underlying research. Read this if you are wondering whether you can share the pack, or if you want the primary sources behind an idea.

    This pack is an original synthesis

    Every document here was written for this library. Nothing in it reproduces text from any third-party book, course, or paid publication. Ideas from other people's work are re-expressed in operational wording — what tends to happen, under what conditions, and what to do about it — and the schools of thought are credited below by name.

    Three constraints held throughout:

    • No third-party text is reproduced. Where an idea originates elsewhere, it is restated, not quoted.
    • No invented evidence. No research finding is expressed as a percentage, a conversion-lift figure, a study citation, or a sample testimonial. Research findings are described qualitatively, because a number nobody can check is worse than no number at all. Numbers that do appear are settings and arithmetic — a saturation value, a contrast ratio, a sample-size formula — not claims about the world.
    • Manipulation is named. Where a technique works by exploiting confusion rather than by helping someone decide, the relevant document says so and marks the line.

    Nothing paid is redistributed here

    No proprietary, licensed, or purchased material is bundled in this repository. If a body of work below interests you, buy it from the people who made it. That is both the honest position and the useful one — the primary sources carry the experimental detail, the caveats, and the citations that a synthesis necessarily compresses.

    The bodies of work this draws on

    • Consumer psychology, applied to marketing and interfaces — notably the published writing of Nick Kolenda on how people process, price, and decide. The strongest debt in this pack is the framing of processing ease, association spillover, and mental simulation as the mechanisms most tactics reduce to.
    • Influence and persuasion research — the tradition associated with Robert Cialdini: reciprocity, social proof, authority, commitment, liking, scarcity, and the conditions under which each holds or backfires.
    • Positioning and brand strategy — the classic literature, including the positioning tradition of Ries and Trout and the brand-equity work of David Aaker, along with the jobs-to-be-done framing associated with Clayton Christensen and Bob Moesta.
    • Behavioural economics and judgement under uncertainty — anchoring, framing, loss aversion, and the limits of each, from the Kahneman and Tversky line of research and its successors.
    • Usability and interaction design — the discount-usability tradition associated with Jakob Nielsen and Don Norman, including heuristic evaluation, small-sample usability testing, and affordance and feedback as design obligations.
    • Direct-response copywriting — the specificity-and-mechanism school running from Claude Hopkins through the modern direct-response writers: concrete claims, stated reasons, testable promises.
    • Web accessibility and performance guidance — the Web Content Accessibility Guidelines and the widely-adopted user-centric performance metrics, which supply the contrast ratios, keyboard requirements, and loading targets used in the build and launch documents.
    • Current search and AI-search practice — search-engine documentation on structured data, indexing, and crawling, alongside the emerging conventions for making content quotable by AI answer engines.
    • Statistics for experimentation — standard treatments of power, minimum detectable effect, and stopping rules, which supply the arithmetic in the measurement document.

    How to read further

    Start with the primary sources for the ideas you intend to lean on hardest. If your brand's problem is belief, read the influence and copywriting sources. If it is comprehension, read the usability sources. If it is being found, read the search engines' own documentation before anything written about it.

    Two cautions worth carrying into any of that reading. First, much published conversion advice was developed on high-arousal retail, and transfers badly to a calm, considered, high-trust purchase — this pack names that dial constantly for exactly that reason. Second, a single study is a single study: prefer findings that have survived replication, and treat any effect size quoted without its conditions as a story rather than a fact.

    Licence and sharing

    This pack is original work and is safe to share, fork, and adapt. If you build on it, keep the honesty rails. They are not decoration — they are the reason the tactics stay usable on a brand that has to survive its own customers.

    Apply it

    • Nothing I add to this pack reproduces text from a paid, licensed, or copyrighted source.
    • Every idea borrowed from the bodies of work above is re-expressed in operational wording and credited by school of thought.
    • No research finding has been introduced as a percentage, lift figure, study citation, or sample testimonial.
    • Research findings are stated qualitatively — what tends to happen, under what conditions.
    • Any tactic that works by exploiting confusion is named as such, with the line marked.
    • Primary sources are bought from their authors, not copied into this repository.
    • Before leaning on any finding, I checked whether it was developed on a purchase like mine, or on a very different one.
